# ON THE SIGNATURE OF ALL THINGS:

->That is/<-

## The description of all things/ how the Internal is signified by the External.

->Written in the year after the<-
->Birth of Christ/ 1622.<-

->By<-
### Jacob Böhme/
->Otherwise known as<-
->The Teutonic PHILOSOPHER. <note>original: "Teutonicum Philosophum." Böhme was famously known as the "Teutonic Philosopher," a title reflecting his German heritage and his profound influence on Western mysticism.</note><-

# Preface

## To the Highly Favorable Reader.

Highly favorable reader / since this book came into my hands without a preface / it is unknown to me whether the author composed a preface for it or not; and the kind-hearted reader should know / that the author’s intention in this book is: To learn to recognize the true foundation of the internal through the external designation <note>original: "eussere bezeichnung"; this refers to the "Signature of Things," the belief that the outward appearance of a plant or mineral reveals its inner spiritual properties</note>: Thus, through the external visible created Being / to reveal and make known the invisible / uncreated Being of all beings <note>original: "Wesen aller Wesen"; a common mystical term for God or the ultimate source of reality</note>: also to open up and make known through the external that which is in itself mute and unrecognized / which

<summary>The translator or editor introduces the work, explaining that the author's purpose is to use the visible, physical world as a key to understanding the invisible, spiritual essence of God and nature.</summary>
<keywords>preface, signature of things, spiritual essence, visible and invisible, mysticism, divine nature</keywords>

Chapter I. How everything that is spoken concerning God is mute and without understanding without the knowledge of the <term>Signature</term> <note>The "Signature" refers to the "Signatura Rerum" or the "Signature of All Things," a doctrine suggesting that every object in the natural world bears an outward sign of its inner spiritual essence.</note>; and how the Signature lies within the human mind according to the <term>Essence of all Essences</term> <note>original: "Wesen aller Wesen." This refers to the fundamental divine substance or the primordial ground of being from which all things originate.</note>.

# ON THE SIGNATURE
## OF THINGS.
### CHAPTER I.

**How everything that is spoken concerning God is mute and without understanding without the knowledge of the Signature: and how the Signature lies within the human mind according to the Essence of all Essences.**

Everything that is spoken, written, or taught concerning God without the knowledge of the <term>Signature</term> <note>The "Signature" refers to the outward manifestation of the inner essence; the idea that the physical form of a thing reveals its spiritual character.</note> is mute and without understanding; for it comes only from a historical delusion, passed from another's mouth, wherein the spirit is mute without knowledge. But if the Spirit opens the Signature to him, then he understands the other's mouth and further understands how the Spirit has revealed itself out of the <term>Essence</term> <note>original: "Essenz"; the fundamental spiritual substance of a thing.</note> through the <term>Principle</term> <note>original: "Principium"; the foundational beginning or governing law.</note> in the resonance of the voice. For though I see that one speaks, teaches, preaches, and writes of God, and I likewise hear and read the same, it is not yet sufficiently understood by me; but if his resonance and his Spirit enter out of his Signature and <term>Formation</term> <note>original: "Gestaldniß"; the specific shape or configuration of a being's nature.</note> into my own Formation, and mark his Formation into mine, then I may understand him in the true ground, whether it be spoken or written—provided he has the hammer that can strike my bells. Therein

<summary>This opening chapter argues that true spiritual understanding requires an internal recognition of the "Signature," or the divine pattern imprinted on all things, rather than mere historical or secondhand learning.</summary>
<keywords>Signature of All Things, divine essence, spiritual resonance, inner knowledge, Jacob Böhme, formation of the spirit</keywords>

# ON THE SIGNATURE

We recognize that all human characteristics come from one source, that they have only a single root and mother; otherwise, one human being could not understand another through sound. For with the sound or speech, the form <note>original: "gestalt"</note> imprints itself into the form of another; a similar sound catches and moves the other. In the sound, the Spirit imprints its own likeness—which it has created in the essence <term>Essence: the underlying spiritual substance of a thing</term> and brought into form in the Beginning <note>original: "Principio"</note>. Thus, one can understand through the word what the Spirit has created for itself, whether in evil or in good. With that same designation, it enters into the form of another person and awakens a similar form in the signature <term>Signature: the external "sign" or physical appearance that reveals the internal character</term> of that other person. Consequently, the forms of both interact <note>original: "inqualiren," meaning to work together or merge qualities</note> within a single form; then there is one shared concept, one will, one spirit, and also one understanding.

And then, secondly, we understand that the signature or form is not the Spirit itself, but rather the container or chest of the Spirit wherein it lies. For the signature stands in the essence and is just like a lute that stands still; it is indeed silent and misunderstood. But if one strikes it, then one understands the form—in what shape and preparation it stands, and according to which voice it is tuned. In the same way, the designation of Nature in its form is a silent being; it is like

<summary>This page explains how human speech and "signatures" allow spirits to communicate by resonating like musical instruments, suggesting a universal spiritual root for all humanity. Boehme uses the metaphor of a lute to describe how the physical form remains silent until "struck" or activated by the spirit.</summary>
<keywords>Signature, Essence, Resonance, Speech, Spirit, Lute Metaphor, Universal Root, Communication</keywords>

# OF THE NATURE OF THINGS, CHAPTER I.

->A prepared lute-play <note>original: "Lautenspiel"; a lute or musical instrument</note>, upon which the spirit of the will strikes; whichever strings he hits, they sound according to their own property. Within the human mind lies the signature <note>The "Signature" refers to the outward mark or character that reveals the inner essence of a thing</note> quite artfully prepared, according to the Being of all beings <note>original: "Wesen aller Wesen"; a term for the Divine Essence or the totality of existence</note>. Nothing more is lacking to man than the skillful Master who can play his instrument—that is, the true Spirit of the high power of Eternity. If that same Spirit is awakened in man, so that it moves in the center of the mind, then it strikes the instrument of the human form. Then the form goes forth with the resonance in the Word from the mouth. Just as his instrument was tuned <note>original: "gezogen"; literally "drawn" or "strung"</note> at the time of his incarnation <note>original: "Menschwerdung"; the process of becoming human or being formed in the womb</note>, so it sounds, and such is his knowledge. The inner reveals itself in the resonance of the Word, for that is the mind’s natural knowledge of itself.

Man indeed has all the forms of all three worlds <note>The three worlds typically refer to the dark fire-world, the light love-world, and the outer visible world</note> lying within him, for he is a complete image of God or of the Being of all beings. Only in his incarnation is the order established within him. For there are three master-builders <note>original: "Werckmeister"</note> in him who prepare his form, namely the threefold *fiat* <note>Latin: "let it be done"; the creative command of God</note>, according to the three worlds. These are in a wrestling match for the form, and the form is then figured according to that wrestling; whichever master retains the upper-regiment <note>The dominant rule or authority</note> and prevails in the essence <note>original: "Essentz"</note>, according to that will the—

As soon as a human being is born into this world, his spirit strikes his instrument; thus, one sees in the external resonance and conduct his internal form <note>original: "gestaltnuß" — the inner configuration or character of a soul</note> in good or evil. For as his instrument sounds, so also do the senses proceed from the essence of the mind <note>original: "gemüt" — the deep-seated disposition or soul-center</note>, and thus the external spirit of the will proceeds with its gestures, as is seen in humans and animals; for there is a great difference in behavior, as one brother or sister does not act like the other.

Furthermore, we must recognize that although one *fiat* <note>original: "fiat" — the creative "let it be" or formative power</note> maintains the upper hand and shapes the form accordingly, the other two nevertheless provide a resonance whenever their instrument is struck. This is seen in how many a person, and also many an animal—whether they are inclined toward great evil or good—is nevertheless moved by a counter-resonance toward evil or good. Such a person often lets his inborn form sink away when the counter-resonance strikes his hidden lute or form. As we see, an evil person is often moved by a good person to rest from his wickedness when the pious person, with his lovely spirit, strikes the other's hidden instrument. Likewise

# OF THINGS, CHAPTER I.

The same likewise happens to the godly man; if the Evil One strikes his hidden instrument with the spirit of his wrath, then the <term>wrath-form</term><note>original: "Zorngestaltnuß"; refers to the latent quality of anger or severity within a person's spiritual makeup</note> is awakened in the godly man. Thus, one is set against the other so that one should be the physician of the other. For as the form of life is—that is, as the life-form is shaped at the time of the <term>Fiat</term><note>Latin: "Let it be"; the creative word of God</note> during the <term>incarnation</term><note>original: "Menschwerdung"; the process of becoming human or taking on flesh</note>—so also is his natural spirit, which he originates out of the <term>essence</term><note>original: "Essenz"; the fundamental spiritual substance</note> of all three <term>principles</term><note>original: "Principien"; the three foundational realms of existence in Böhme's cosmology</note>. And such a will he also carries out of his own property.

But now, his will may be broken; for when a stronger one comes over him and awakens his inner form with his introduced sound and will-spirit, then his <term>superior sovereignty</term><note>original: "Oberregiment"; the governing part of the soul or mind</note> loses its right and power. We see this in the power of the sun: how it, with its might, <term>qualifies</term><note>original: "qualificiret"; to transform or imbue with a specific quality</note> a bitter and sour fruit into sweetness and loveliness.

In the same way, a good man may be corrupted among an evil crowd; likewise, a good herb on a bad field cannot sufficiently manifest its true <term>virtue</term><note>original: "Tugend"; here meaning the inherent healing power or medicinal property of a plant</note>. For in the good man, the hidden evil instrument is awakened, and in the herb, a contrary essence is awakened from the earth, so that often the good is transformed into an evil, and the evil into a good; and as it now stands in the power...

# ON THE SIGNATURE

Whatever quality dwells within, so it marks itself outwardly in its external form and shape. A human being is marked in their speech, will, and conduct, as well as by the form of their limbs, which they possess and must use according to that same internal shape. Their inner configuration also marks them in the features of the face; likewise an animal, and also a herb and even trees. Every single thing is marked externally exactly as it is internally. For even if it happens that a thing often transforms from evil into good, or from good into evil, it still retains its external <term>Character<note>original: "Caracter"; here meaning a spiritual or physical seal or stamp of identity</note></term>, so that one may recognize the good or the evil—that is, the transformation. 

For a human being is recognized in this regard by their daily practices, as well as by their behavior and words; for the highest instrument is always struck, whichever one is most tightly strung. It is the same with an animal: if it is evil but has been tamed by force and drawn toward another property, it does not easily let its first inherent form be noticed—unless that form is provoked, in which case it steps forth before all other forms. 

It is also the same with the herbs of the earth: if a herb is sown from an evil field into a good one, it immediately acquires a strong body, as well as a lovely scent and power, and displays its inner <term>Essence<note>original: "Essenz"; the fundamental nature or "being" of the plant</note></term> in the outward appearance. And there is nothing in <term>Nature<note>original: "Natur"</note></term> that is created

# OF THE NATURE OF THINGS, CHAPTER I.

We recognize this in the power and formation of this world, how the single Being <note>original: "einige wesen," referring to the unified divine essence</note> has revealed itself in a likeness through its birth-giving within desire. It has revealed itself in as many forms and shapes as we see and recognize in the stars and elements, as well as in the creatures, and even in the trees and herbs. Therefore, the greatest understanding lies in the Signature <term>Signature: the outward physical marks or features of an object that reveal its hidden inner character or medicinal use</term>, wherein man (as the image of the greatest virtue) not only learns to know himself, but he may also therein learn to recognize the Being of all Beings. For by the external formation of all creatures, by their drive and desire, and likewise by their outgoing sound, voice, and speech, one recognizes the hidden Spirit. For nature has given every thing its speech (according to its essence and formation); for from the essence originates the speech or the sound. From that same essence comes the quality of the essence: in the outgoing sound or power, the living quality is in the sound, and the essential quality is in the scent, power, and form. Every thing has its mouth for revelation, and that is the Language of Nature <term>Language of Nature: the idea that the physical world speaks a direct, universal language of form and sound that reveals its divine origin</term>, out of which every thing speaks from its own property, and always reveals itself, and

### CHAPTER II.

#### Concerning the Opposition and the Conflict in the Being of All Beings.

Since the forms are so many and manifold, where each one brings forth a different will out of its own property than the other, we understand herein the opposition and the conflict in the being of all beings <note>original: "Wesen aller Wesen"; a central concept in Böhme's philosophy referring to the ultimate source of all existence</note>. We see how each one treats the other as an enemy, poisoning and killing it—that is, overcoming its essence and the spirit of that essence, and leading it into another form. From this, sickness and pain arise when one essence breaks another.

And then we also understand herein the Medicine <note>original: "Artzney"</note>: how one thing heals another and brings it to health. If this were not so, there would be no Nature, but rather an eternal stillness and no Will. For it is the counter-will <note>original: "Widerwille"</note> that creates movement and the origin <note>original: "Vhrstand"</note> of seeking, so that the opposing torment <note>original: "Quall"; Böhme uses this term to describe both a source of pain and a source of "welling up" or vital energy</note> seeks rest, and in that seeking only elevates and further ignites itself. 

It is for us to understand how the Physician <note>original: "Arizt"</note> stands in the likeness of every thing; for in likeness arises the fulfillment of the will as its highest joy. For every thing desires a will of its own—

<summary>This page explores the philosophical origins of conflict and disease, arguing that the "counter-will" between different essences is necessary for the movement of nature and the practice of medicine.</summary>
<keywords>Signature, Essence, Conflict of Beings, Origin of Sickness, Nature of Medicine, Counter-will, Jacob Böhme, Alchemy</keywords>

--- Page 18 ---
<meta>This page continues the philosophical exploration of the struggle between opposing forces in nature and the search for harmony. It follows the previous discussion on the origins of conflict within the natural order.</meta>

# OF THINGS, CHAPTER II.

its own kind; and by aversion it is sickened, but if it receives a will of its own kind, then it rejoices in that equality and sinks therein into rest. Thus, out of enmity, a joy is made; for eternal nature has born nothing out of itself in its desire but an equality. And were there not an everlasting mixing <note>The author refers to the constant interaction and friction of different elemental forces</note>, there would be an eternal peace in nature. But in that case, nature would not be manifest; it becomes manifest in strife, so that every thing raises itself up and desires to flee from the strife into the still rest, and yet by that very act runs into another, and thereby only awakens the strife.

Thus we find clearly in the light of nature that there is no better remedy for adversity, and it has no higher physician than freedom. That is the light of nature, that is the desire of the spirit. And then we find that the essence <note>original: "Essenz"</note> cannot be better helped than with equality; for the essence is a being <note>original: "Wesen"</note>, and its desire stands toward being. Thus every taste <note>The sensory or spiritual quality of a substance</note> desires only its own kind, and when it receives that, its hunger is fulfilled and stilled; it ceases to hunger and rejoices in itself. From this, the sickness sinks into a rest within itself, for the hunger of adversity ceases to interact <note>original: "qualificiren" - a technical term meaning to operate or exert influence through specific qualities</note>.

<summary>This page explains that while strife is necessary for nature to be manifest, true healing and rest are found when a being encounters its own likeness and achieves a state of equality. The author identifies freedom and the "light of nature" as the ultimate physicians for the conflicts inherent in existence.</summary>

# ON THE SIGNATURE
<note>original: "DE SIGNATURA" - Latin title for "On the Signature of All Things," a central concept in Böhme's philosophy where the inner essence of a thing is revealed in its outer form.</note>

Since human life stands in three <term>Principles</term><note>Böhme uses "Principles" to describe the three fundamental realms of reality: the dark fire-world, the light love-world, and the physical world.</note>, as in a threefold <term>Essence</term>, and also possesses a threefold Spirit from the property of each Essence—namely, according to eternal Nature, according to the property of the **fire**; and secondly, according to the property of the eternal **Light** and the **Divine Being**; and thirdly, according to the property of the **outer world**—we must therefore consider the properties of both this threefold Spirit and the threefold Essence and will: how each Spirit, together with its Essence, leads itself into conflict and sickness, and what its cure and medicine may be.

We understand that outside of Nature there is an eternal stillness and rest, as the **Nothing**; and then we understand that in the eternal Nothing, an eternal **Will** originates to lead the Nothing into Something, so that the Will may find, feel, and behold itself. For in the Nothing, the Will would not be manifest to itself; thus we recognize that the Will seeks itself and finds itself within itself. Its seeking is a **Desire**, and its finding is the substance of the Desire, wherein the Will finds itself. It finds nothing but the property of **hunger**, which it is itself; this it draws into itself—that is, it draws itself into itself and finds itself within itself. And this drawing-inward creates within it a shadowing or **Darkness**, which does not exist in the <term>Freedom</term><note>original: "Freyheit" - Böhme's term for the "Ungrund" or the absolute abyss of God before manifestation.</note> as in the Nothing; for

<summary>Böhme explains that human life is composed of three principles (fire, light, and the physical world) and describes how the divine "Will" emerges from the "Nothing" through desire to create self-awareness.</summary>

# OF THINGS, CHAPTER II.

For the sake of **Freedom**, the Will overshadows itself with the essence of **Desire**; for it is Desire that creates essence <note>The author uses "essence" or "being" (Wesen) to describe the first step toward physical manifestation.</note>, and not the Will. Since the Will must therefore stand in darkness with its desire, this constitutes its **Opposition**. Thus, it creates within itself another will to go out from the darkness back into Freedom—as if into the **Nothing**—and yet it cannot reach Freedom outside of itself. For Desire goes out from itself and creates **Torment** and **Darkness**. 

Therefore, the Will (understand here the secondary or created will) must go into itself. Yet there is no separation, for within itself, before the Desire, is Freedom as the Nothing. However, the Will cannot be a Nothing, for it desires to manifest itself within the Nothing. Yet no manifestation can occur except through the essence of Desire. 

The more the re-captured Will desires manifestation, the more and the more severely the Desire draws into itself. Within itself, it creates **three forms**: 

1.  **Desire**, which is **Astringency** <note>original: "Herbe." This refers to a sharp, contracting, or harsh quality that pulls inward.</note>, and it produces **Hardness**, for it is an enclosing, from which **Coldness** originates. 
2.  The **Drawing** <note>The internal tension or attraction.</note> creates the **Sting** or **Stirring** within the hardness—an enmity against the astringent, contracted hardness. This Drawing is the **second form**, and a cause of **Movement** and **Life**. 
3.  It moves within the Astringency and Hardness, which the Hardness, as the enclos- <insert>ing</insert>

# OF ALL THINGS, CHAPTER II.

From these two, as if from such an Essence <note>original: "Essenz"; here referring to the fundamental substance of being</note> as from a mother, the Spirit takes its origin. This Spirit contains both properties within itself and is a son of these properties, and also a Lord over them. For within the Spirit stands the power; it may awaken whichever property it desires. These properties reside within the Essence and are like a prepared life, or like an instrument with many voices which stand silent. And the Spirit, as that which proceeds forth, is the true Life; it may strike the instrument however it wishes, whether in evil or in good, according to love or according to wrath. And as the Spirit strikes, and as the instrument sounds, so is it received by its resonance <note>original: "Gegenhall"; literally a counter-echo or reflection</note> as if by its own likeness.

If the voice of Love—as the desire for Freedom—is struck, then the echo is captured by that same Freedom and the delight of love; for it is similar to its taste and the desire of its will; one equal delight catches the other. So it is also the same regarding enmity and reluctance: if the instrument is struck according to the desire for Nature <note>original: "Natur"; here meaning the harsh, material, or fallen state of existence</note>, such as in fury, wrath, and bitter falsehood, then that same resonance and furious desire captures it; for it is of its own property and a fulfillment of its hunger. # ON THE SIGNATURE

The first son <note>In Böhme’s cosmology, this refers to the first movement or "out-going" of the divine will</note> goes out from the **revelation** back into itself, into **freedom**; for in **nature** it has become an eternal life, and yet it does not possess nature essentially, but dwells within itself and passes through nature as a shining light. And the first will goes out of itself: for it is the desire for revelation; it seeks itself outside of itself, and yet it grasps the desire within itself. It desires to lead the inner out of itself. Thus it has two properties: by seeking within itself, it creates the **center of nature**; for it is like a poison in the will, a terrible elevation like a flash of lightning and a thunderclap. For this desire only craves **anguish** and to be terrible, to find itself within itself, moving from **Nothing** into **Something**. 

And the other form goes out of itself like a terror or a born resonance; for it is not the desire of the first will to remain in terrible death, but only to lead itself out of the Nothing in this way and to find itself. And we understand by the "center within itself"—with its elevating fierceness and the fierce will toward nature—the **dark world**; and by the "going out of itself" toward revelation, the **outer world**; and by the other will from the first, which enters back into freedom, the **light-world** or the kingdom of joy or the **true Deity**. The desire of the dark world is toward revelation, as toward the outer world.

# OF THINGS, CHAPTER III.

Writings on divine revelation have for a long time explained the nature of Divine Wisdom and the divine, eternal, and unfathomable Birth. Now we shall turn ourselves toward the external worldly being, as a revelation of the Eternal—namely to the metals, herbs, and trees, as well as to humans and animals.

We see that the metals have a different body <note>original: "Corpus"</note> than the living creatures, or than the earth and stones are; thus Reason <note>original: "Vernunfft"</note> asks how the origin <note>original: "vhrstand"</note> of each thing came to be, since in the beginning everything arose from one Mother, and yet Eternity has no temporal beginning. There we must once again consider the Mother of the first Progenitress, and how one being separates itself from another. Just as the beginning-like separates from the eternal, or time from Eternity, yet one stands within the other; but it divides into two principles <note>original: "Principia"</note>, namely into God’s Kingdom and the kingdom of this world. And yet, all is God’s will. However, Christ calls the Devil a Prince of this world; we can also say in what manner he is a prince, and also say that this world is not his property, but rather that he is the poorest creature in this world, and indeed not in this world at all. So, look upon the first ground, upon the Mother, who has given birth to all creatures as well as the earth, stones, and all metals.

The properties of all things reside in the spiritual <term>Sulphur, Mercury, and Salt</term> <note>In Boehme’s thought, these are not just physical chemicals, but the three primary forces of all reality: expansion, contraction, and the resulting stability.</note>, and everything that has begun has arisen in and out of their <term>Impression</term> <note>The process by which spiritual forces "press" themselves into physical form.</note>. From the beginning, it proceeded through the first form of the Mother—namely, through the harsh drawing-inward—and entered into a created being through the <term>fiat</term> <note>Latin: "let it be done"; the creative word of God.</note>. This gives rise to many kinds of essence and spirit according to the primary property of the <term>Separation</term> <note>The "Scheidung" or differentiation of the unified divine light into various qualities.</note>.

These possessed the properties of both eternal worlds within themselves. Those who, after their embodiment, remained with their desire in the property of free delight and directed their will out of the fire and into the light, became **Angels**. But the others, who directed their desire back into the Center—that is, into the harsh and rigorous properties—became **Devils**. These were cast out from the free delight and from the light, as has been reported in other writings.

Therefore, the devils possess neither the Kingdom of God nor the kingdom of this world; for in the beginning of creation, this world was created out of both inner properties <note>The "inner properties" refer to the light/love and the dark/wrath of God.</note>. After the creation of the high spirits, God created this visible world with stars and elements as an out-birth from the eternal Mother of all Beings. All of this proceeded from the eternal Beginning and took on a temporal beginning. For here we must consider that the eternal Birth-giver <note>original: "Gebährerin" — referring to the divine creative matrix or "Sophia" in Böhme's thought</note> moved herself and ignited her forms, whereby one thing became corporeal within the other. However, after God had created the Earth, we are to consider it thus, etc.

The first desire toward Nature impresses itself, and through this Impression, it leads itself into three forms: namely into **Sulphur**, **Mercury**, and **Salt** <note>These are the "Tria Prima," the three primary chemical principles which Böhme uses as metaphors for spiritual and physical forces</note>. Through this Impression, everything becomes substantial and movable—which is not the case in the "Still Nothing"—and it drives itself into the highest Anguish, even unto the "Salnitric" <term>Salnitrisch: a term for the explosive, lightning-like flash of divine or natural energy</term> crack or terror. At that point is the origin of the Fire; thus the "Quall" <note>original: "Quall" — a term Böhme uses to describe the "welling up" or "boiling" of life-force</note> goes on, swirling within itself like the boiling of water by fire. For the harsh desire pulls toward itself, and the fiery desire presses outward, and that is a **Sulphur**. Thus the harsh pulling is a fierce sting, like a breaking, and yet it is held by the Severity so that it cannot give way; thus it is, and it creates pain, just like turning or boiling, which is yet only Spirit without Essence.

Being is—this occurs in Mercury <note>original: "Mercurio"; here referring to the archetypal transformative force in alchemy, not just the metal</note>, and is the form of Mercury itself. Yet, there is no severing or departing from one another; rather, they go together through the kindling of the fire, through the salt-nitrous <term>shriek</term> <note>original: "Salnitrischen schräck"; a sudden, violent chemical reaction or "crack" that Böhme believes triggers the transition from darkness to light</note>. There, with the kindling of the fire, the dying occurs within the fury of the fire, where the <term>torment</term> <note>original: "Quall"; refers to the agonizing tension of the lower forces before they are transformed</note> dies away. And yet it is no death, but a likeness of death; and yet the true eternal and temporal death is thus: There, Freedom seizes itself within itself, and death or the "shriek" falls into the midst of Freedom as if powerless, and yields itself up. Thus the Spirit, as the torment, becomes material (understand this as the very sharp, fiery, anxious torment) and retains only an essential working, like a powerless desire. And in the kindling of the fire in the salt-nitrous shriek, every property separates into itself, and the whole <term>Matter</term> <note>original: "Materia"</note> becomes a particularity, such as metals, stones, and earths. ### CHAPTER IV.

#### Concerning the Birth of the Four Elements and Stars, in their Metallic and Created Properties.

As reported above, all things proceed from a single Mother and separate into two beings according to the law of eternity: into a mortal and an immortal, into life and death, into spirit and body. The spirit is the life, and the body is the death, serving as a house for the spirit. Just as the Holy Trinity stands in its birth, so also the out-birth <note>original: "Auß-geburth"; a term used by Boehme to describe the physical manifestation or "projection" of spiritual realities</note> in Heaven is both substance and spirit, as we see the figure in the outer world, where four elemen-

# SIGNATURE

Elements exist, and yet there is only a single one, which divides itself into four properties: namely Fire, Air, Water, and Earth, as previously mentioned. For we must consider the creation of this world in such a way that the entire essence of eternity moved itself within the space <note>original: "in loco"</note> of this world, and the entire form was ignited and stirred; and this occurred within the Desire for manifestation. There, the birthing process—within the terror <note>The "Schrack" or "Schreck" is a pivotal concept in Boehme's thought, representing the sudden flash or "crack" of transition from darkness to light</note> of the ignited fire—separated into four parts: Fire, Water, and Earth; and the Air is its weaving, outgoing Spirit, as can be observed in Sulfur <term>Sulphur: in alchemy, the active, solar principle of combustion and soul</term>, which consists of these four things.

In the same way, the Stars <note>original: "Gestirne," referring to the celestial influences and constellations</note> were also born from the first Mother, and everything is together like one Body. Everything originates from the inner Spirit, just as a hand or foot grows out from the inner Center <note>original: "Centro"</note>, and already possesses its structural house within the Center as its first effect, and thus grows only in a form that matches the Spirit.

The first Mother of all things—namely the Longing <note>original: "lust"</note> combined with the Desire—introduces herself primarily into seven Forms, and yet remains standing in only three; however, she reveals herself in a sevenfold manner.

The first Form is "harsh" <note>original: "herbe," implying astringency or contraction</note>, like a severe drawing-inward; this is the cause of coldness, of Salt <term>Salt: the alchemical principle of materiality, contraction, and solidification</term>, and of all corporeality.

The second <term>Form</term> <note>German: *Gestalt*. In this context, it refers to a fundamental quality or "property" of being.</note> is the **Sting**, acting as the pull or movement; it causes feeling, as well as standing, aching, and the movement of bitterness, hostility, joy, and suffering.

The third Form is the **Great Anguish** within the <term>Impression</term> <note>The compression or "drawing-in" of spiritual forces into matter.</note>, which causes two Wills: one toward the Fire, toward the highest sharpness; and one toward dying in the Fire, where the Will of free desire sinks into the Fury within the Fire, returns into itself, and creates a radiance within the sharpness of the Fire.

The fourth Form is now the **Fire** itself, acting as the first <term>Principle</term> <note>Latin: *Principium*. The fundamental source or beginning of life.</note> in life, by which the Dark World and Light World are separated. Also, within this same "terror-crack" <note>The sudden flash or shock of transition from darkness to light.</note>, all material separation occurs, and corporeality begins. Here also begins the multiplication according to the property of the first eternal Mind: according to its essence, it is a mortal thing; but according to the **Fire-Torment** <note>German: *Fewerquall*. The agonizing, burning energy that drives life.</note>, it is a living thing.

The fifth Form is now the **Second Desire**, which occurs after the separation. This happens according to two properties: first, according to the desire for Freedom, arising from the Light, which is the highest desire for Love; and second, according to the desire of the Fire, which leads its life and its <term>Essence</term> <note>Latin: *Essenz*.</note> into the love within the Light. The seventh form arises out of all the others, and is the body and dwelling-house or food of the others; for this happens in this way: When the other forms, in penetrating one another, taste each other in the desire of love, then a hunger or desire for love—as for the light—arises in every form. And if she does not get it, then the child cannot get it either; now it suffocates in hunger, or is corrupted in a limb from which the hunger originated.

Mercury <note>original: "Mercurius"</note> is the master-craftsman <term>Werckmeister: the active agent or architect of the work</term> of this Child, which Saturn <note>original: "Saturnus"</note> conceals; when he receives it into his hunger, he casts away his black cloak and rejoices in it. But he is too malicious in the fire-wrath (in his foreign fire, and yet not foreign); when the cloak is gone, he needs only his own fire; he devours the Child and makes it entirely into his own property. When he hungers most fiercely in the fire, one must give him the Sun <note>original: "Sol"</note> (she is his wife) so that his hunger may be stilled. Then, when he is satisfied, he works in the Matter <note>original: "Materia"</note> of the Child with his own hunger or fire, and fulfills his satisfied desire out of the Sun’s property, which he had previously eaten into himself; and he nourishes the Child until it takes on all four elements with the stars <note>original: "Gestirne"</note>, and he becomes heavily pregnant with the Child. Then it belongs in a (foreign fire and yet not foreign) earnest-fire; thus the Father gives it the soul, as the fire-spirit and its first mother, which Mercury ate into himself in his hunger, which is fixed and perfect. As for the spirit of the soul, as the light-life, then the dead arises (and it is the Tincture <note>original: "Tinctur"</note>, which tints or transforms the body).

# ON THE SIGNATURE

And the Child is born, and is henceforth its own being, a Child of Freedom. It asks nothing more of its master-builder <note>original: "Werckmeister," referring to the creative or demiurgic force</note>; it is better than its Father, but no better than its Mother, in whose seed it lay before the Father worked within it. It treads upon the head of the fiery essence of its Father, as if upon the head of the Serpent, and passes freely through Death in the fire. If you do not understand this here, then you are not born into the highest knowledge of <term>spagyry</term> <note>The art of alchemical separation and recombination, specifically the "royal art" of medicine</note>.

Furthermore, we must consider the degrees: how Freedom, as eternal delight, gives hunger to the other forms in the <term>Sulphur</term> <note>In Boehme’s thought, Sulphur represents the "burning desire" or the substance of the soul's will</note>. In the property of the other planets, the shape of the birth is like a turning wheel; this is caused by <term>Mercurius</term> <note>The principle of movement, communication, and vital force</note> within the <term>Sulphur</term>.

The birth of the highest degree turns itself about like Desire, for this world is round, and so too is the birth. After Freedom has given its highest delight (as a golden hunger) to <term>Saturn</term> <note>The principle of contraction, cold, and materialization</note> and has appointed <term>Mercurius</term> as the master-builder, it then composes itself within its own desire according to the property of gentleness. For the first formation of the Golden Child occurs according to the property of the Kingdom of Joy. This, however, happens out of goodness and gentleness, and yields itself to <term>Luna</term> <note>The Moon, representing the physical body or the receptive, feminine principle</note>; for there is a sinking down because of the gentleness, which <term>Mercurius</term> also seizes and works—

Love proceeds out of the fierceness and exists in humility or "sinking." Thus, it has also stepped into an order within the Creation; for this reason, Venus stands on the Wheel around the line of Mars beneath the Sun. For such is the separation in Nature, and thus does one thing proceed from the other. Its metal is copper.

The origin is this: that Love is a desire, and yet it desires only light and joy. Therefore, the metal of Venus <note>Copper</note> is closely related to Gold because of its own quality derived from Freedom; but Mars makes it too fierce and too brittle. Because it separates itself from the fire of Mars, it retains a large portion of the Martian quality within itself.

Copper separates itself from Iron during the birthing process, for it originates from the will of Venus. There is a distinction between them, like that between Body and Soul; for Mars is the fire-soul of Venus and causes Venus to become corporeal. Otherwise, according to her own quality during the "mortification" <note>The alchemical stage of breaking down a substance</note> in the saltpeter-shock, Venus yields only water. # OF MATTERS CHAPTER IV.

Whatever the first desire—through the impression in Saturn—makes into the fierce evil of Mars, the Sun makes good again. Just as the Divine Sun tinctures <note>Tincturing refers to the alchemical process of transformation or "dyeing" one substance with the quality of another.</note> the wrath or anger of God so that a kingdom of joy arises out of the fierce property of God's wrath, so too does the outer Sun tincture the outer Sulfur, namely Saturn and Mars. This creates a laudable temperament—seen as greening and growth—in all metals and creatures. Therefore, the Sun is the Center (which human reason refuses to believe) within the planetary wheel and in all growing and living things.

### C H A P T E R  V.

**Concerning Sulphurous death, and how the deceased body becomes living again and is restored to its first glory.**

All life and movement—including understanding, reason, and the senses—both in living and growing things, has its origin in Sulfur; specifically in the desire of Nature and in the desire for the delight of Freedom.

In the desire of Nature, death and enclosure arise; but in the desire of Freedom, the unlocking and Life arise. For the desire of Freedom tinctures the desire of dark Nature, so that the fierce Mother <note>The "fierce Mother" refers to the harsh, contracting forces of material nature before they are enlightened.</note> lets her own law sink away and [yields] herself to Freedom.

<summary>This page describes the Sun's role as a transformative "tincture" that turns the harsh qualities of Saturn and Mars into life, and introduces the dual nature of Sulfur in the process of death and rebirth.</summary>
<keywords>Alchemy, Sun, Saturn, Mars, Sulfur, Tincture, Divine Wrath, Planetary Wheel, Rebirth, Nature vs. Freedom</keywords>

--- Page 71 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion on the nature of spiritual and physical birth, following the previous page's focus on the origins of desire.</meta>

# ON THE SIGNATURE

One gives desire to freedom; thus life grows in death, for without light there is no life. If the light in the essence of the <term>Sulphur</term><note>In Boehme’s thought, Sulphur represents the expansive, fiery, and vitalizing principle of nature.</note> is extinguished, then it is an eternal death. For no one can make anything living unless God moves Himself in the desire for delight within that very death. Death cannot take life into itself unless the first desire—the desire for free delight—reveals itself within the desire for nature, wherein the enclosure known as death is born.

Therefore, when man died in the Sulphur, no one could make him living again unless the free delight—the desire for eternal life—entered again into his Sulphur, into the birth of the nature of the human property. It had to move the enclosed death, which is the <term>Center of Nature</term>, and give itself back into that center, into the property of the soul and into the soul’s essence and corporeality; and this is exactly what has happened. We know that the true Sulphur is a birthing of all spirituality and corporeality, insofar as its first origin is concerned. Where it is heavenly, it is the birthing of the <term>Being of all Beings</term><note>original: "Wesen aller Wesen," a common mystical term for God as the underlying reality of all things.</note>, for everything lies in this birth—whatever eternity and time contain, possess, or are capable of. Now, however, according to the kingdom of this world, it is also earthly, as a figure of the Eternal. For within it stands time and the <term>creature</term><note>Referring to all created things or beings.</note>, and everything that is visible and invisible.

# OF THINGS, CHAPTER V.

Now man, together with all life, was created and born according to the kingdom of this world out of the outer *Sulphur* <note>In alchemy, Sulphur represents the active, combustible, and soul-like principle of substance.</note>; man out of the inner and the outer, and the outer creature solely out of the outer. For man is God’s image and likeness, and the other creatures are a likeness according to the configuration in the internal birthing within God’s Wisdom—as in the expressed or out-born heavenly Being, according to both eternal Principles.

Now, however, man was good and entirely perfect, created according to and out of all three worlds, as an image of the Deity in whom God dwells. And he was the very Being itself—what God is according to eternity and according to time in all three worlds—but a creature with a beginning according to the nature of a creature. And he died to the heavenly and Divine Being through desire. For the inner desire, which was born in the *Center* <note>original: "Centro"</note> as if in the fire, wherein the life stood in the Divine Essence—that is, it ignited the essence of the Divine Gentleness <note>original: "Sanfftmuth," a key term in Boehme's theology referring to the soft, light-filled quality of the divine heart.</note> wherein the joyful or Angelic form stands—that desire turned itself away from the inner desire of Freedom and Eternity in Time, as if into the Out-birth <note>The "Out-birth" refers to the physical, manifested world produced from the spiritual realm.</note>, into the planetary property; out of the pure Divine Element into the four elements. Thus, the inner Divine—

--- Page 73 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of the Fall of Man and the resulting chemical and spiritual transformation of the human body from a divine to a temporal state.</meta>

# THE SIGNATURE

The Divine Essence—or inner corporeality—no longer had a guide or life; that was the "dying." For the soul-fire, originating from the Father’s property <note>In Böhme’s theosophy, the "Father" represents the harsh, fiery foundation of nature, while the "Son" represents light and love.</note>, turned away from the Son’s property, in which alone the Divine Life resides.

Thus the soul’s property remained bare, existing only with its will in the external *Sulphur* <term>Sulphur: here referring to the combustible, energetic principle of material nature</term>, while the inner [essence] faded and remained standing in eternal immobility, as if in an eternal nothingness, wherein there was no longer any accomplishment. Thus, man lived with his external body merely for the sake of Time alone. The noble gold of the heavenly corporeality, which was meant to tincture <term>Tincture: to permeate or transform a lower substance into a higher one, like alchemy transforming lead to gold</term> the external body, had faded. Consequently, the external body remained standing solely in the life of nature’s desire—that is, in the property of the soul-fire. Understand this as being in the form and property of Mars <note>original: "Martis gestalt"; Mars represents the bitter, fiery, and aggressive quality in Böhme’s planetary correspondences</note>, as in the wrath of God. This wrath in the *Sulphur* is the property of God’s anger and the dark world. 

But because the external body was created out of Time, Time—in the form of the stars and the elements—immediately took over the governance within him. For the divine property (the Desire of the Deity which governs and tinctures Time so that a holy life might emerge from the creature of Time) had faded; its own love in the divine desire turned to water. Thus, his fire now rules within a water.

# OF THE NATURE OF THINGS, CHAPTER V.

Water; and it was, in the face of God’s will and desire, pale and dead, and the soul had to make do with the light of the sun. Since time has a beginning and an end, and the will, through its desire, has surrendered itself to the temporal guide, the government of time eventually breaks its constructed spirit; thus the body also dies and passes away. And that is what God said to Adam: that he should not eat of the tree or the growth of the knowledge of Good and Evil—of both qualities—or he would die. And so it happened: he died in the <term>Sulphur</term><note>In Boehme’s alchemy, Sulphur represents the "burning" or "active" principle of life and desire.</note>; the *Sul* <note>original: "Sul"; Boehme often breaks words into syllables to find mystical meanings, here implying the soul's essence or "Sol/Sun".</note> died to the Kingdom of God, which is the delight of God’s freedom from which the Divine Light shines, and in which Divine Love burns as the beloved fire.

Now, there was no remedy unless God’s desire entered again into the deceased Sulphur—that is, into his dead *Sul*, into the deceased essence—and ignited it again with the fire of love. This occurred in Christ; there, the heavenly body, in which God’s light shines, rose again. But for this to happen, the desire of love had to enter again into the desire of the ignited wrath, and extinguish and overcome the wrath with love. The Divine Water had to enter again into the burning fire of the soul, and extinguish the grim death within the harsh <term>fiat</term><note>Latin: "let it be done." Here referring to the creative word or the formative power of nature.</note>, which acts as the desire toward nature.

<summary>Jacob Boehme explains the spiritual death of Adam as a loss of divine light within the soul's "Sulphur" and describes how Christ restores life by extinguishing the fire of God's wrath with the water of divine love.</summary>

# OF THINGS, CHAPTER V.

He who has given life alone can take it away again; and He who first made it corporeal must swallow it back into Himself and transmute it into another body. This is the <term>Sulphur</term> <note>In alchemy, Sulphur represents the active, combustible, and masculine principle of soul and fire.</note>, which holds <term>Mercury</term> <note>Mercury represents the fluid, volatile, and feminine principle of spirit and mediation.</note> within itself as its master craftsman; it must take it back from its belly in the dark <term>Saturn</term> <note>Saturn refers to the leaden, dark, or "prima materia" state of matter—the beginning of the work.</note>, in the *fiat* <note>Latin: "let it be done," referring to the divine word of creation.</note>, and lead it into its own fire. With its own fire, it must separate the four elements from it and set them into one. Just as God, on the Last Day, will separate the essence of the four elements from the pure element in His own fire during the Great Ignition, so that eternal corporeality may begin in the pure element—and just as in the death of a human being, the four elements are separated from the true human (who is the element of God), and the heavenly body remains alone in itself—so it also happens in the transmutation of metals.

Life is revealed: But its mother is not recognized in it from the outside, unless one provokes its master-builder <note>original: "Werckmeister"; referring to the internal creative force or soul-architect</note> with its own malice. Yet even this cannot be done unless one introduces a foreign malice, by which its own is provoked. Then (if its wrath is enraged) it becomes so hungry and thirsty, and yet can find no refreshment within itself; so it attacks its master-builder who made it, and struggles against its Creator <note>original: "Schöpffer"</note>, just as the earthly evil man struggles against God. This continues until it devours and consumes itself, just as a poison from fire consumes the body, unless someone comes to its aid and stills its hunger. Yet no one can still this terrible hunger except God Himself who made it; and if He does not come to its aid at the right time, then the hunger in its fury consumes the body and places it into the eternal darkness.

This hunger desires nothing but God's mercy <note>original: "Barmhertzigkeit"</note>, that it might be delivered from the anguish of hell <note>original: "Höllen angst"</note>. However, it cannot achieve this within itself, for it is locked within the wrath of God; likewise, its dear mother who suckled it in the beginning is also locked in death. But if God shows it His grace and gives to it again of His love, then the wrath is terrified before the love,

# OF THINGS, CHAPTER V.

Then the old Adam becomes sick and weak, entirely dark and black, and dies away; then the **four elements** depart from him with their colors. The Master-Builder lets him continually pass away and works constantly on the **new body**, which is to rise from death; and no one sees his work, for he works in the dark.

But the Artist does not take charge of the work himself, but only gives the Master-Builder his own food, until he sees that the <term>vegetative life</term> <note>The "vegetabilische Leben" refers to the basic power of growth and nourishment that precedes conscious life.</note> shows itself with new color out of the blackness in the dark death; then the **new man** is ready. Then the Artist comes and brings the **soul**, and gives it to the Master-Builder: at this the Master-Builder is startled, that another life is within him.

### CHAPTER VI.

How water and oil are birthed, and on the distinction between water and oil, and on vegetable life and growth.

All life, growth, and movement consist of two things: namely, in the *Delight* <note>original: "Lust" – in this context, a divine, free joy or inclination rather than carnal desire</note>, and then in the *Desire* <note>original: "Begierde" – a driving, hungry longing or attraction</note>; the Delight is a free will, and compared to Nature it is to be regarded as a Nothing; but the Desire is a hunger. In the Desire arises the driving spirit, known as the natural spirit, and in the Delight arises the supernatural spirit, which nevertheless belongs to Nature, yet not out of its own property, but out of the property of the Desire. The Desire is the driving of the Essence <term>Essence: the fundamental "being" or substance of a thing</term> acting as a hunger, and the Delight is the being of that hunger which it grasps within itself; for the Desire is only a hungry will, and is the natural spirit in its forms—

<meta>catchword: forms—</meta>

--- Page 80 ---
<meta>This page continues the philosophical discussion of the divine nature, specifically how the "free will" of God interacts with the "desire" of nature to create manifestation.</meta>

# OF THINGS, CHAPTER VI.

Forms; but the Delight <note>original: "Lust" – in this context, a joyful, divine inclination or pleasure</note> arises out of freedom, for God is without desire, insofar as His own being—as far as He is called God—is concerned. For He needs nothing; everything is His, and He Himself is everything. But He has a Will of Delight, and is Himself that same Will, to manifest Himself in that Delight. Yet, no manifestation can occur in the free, emotionless Delight, for it is without desire; it is as if it were nothing compared to Nature, and yet it is everything. But it is not according to desire, as in Nature, but according to the fulfillment of Nature. It is the fulfillment of the hungry desire that is Nature; it gives itself freely into the hunger of Nature, for it is a Spirit without substance <note>original: "Wesen" – can mean being, essence, or substance</note> and desire, entirely free as a "Nothing." 

But the desire makes it into a substance within itself, and this according to two properties: one according to the eternal Freedom, which is free from torment <note>original: "quall" – the agonizing tension of existence</note>, and the other according to the desire, which gives a vegetative <term>vegetabilisch: a life-force that grows and expands like a plant</term> life, like an awakening or a pouring-forth of oneself. 

The free substance is and gives an Oil, and the property of desire gives a life to the Oil. The Oil is a Light, and the property of desire gives an essence to the Light, acting as the fiery property, so that the Light becomes a shine—as one sees in fire and light. And yet the free Delight remains in itself a free will, but gives its Gentleness as a yieldedness <note>original: "gelassenheit" – a central term in German mysticism referring to the state of letting go or surrendering to God</note>.

# ON THE SIGNATURE

->**Serenity**<- <note>original: "Gelassenheit" – a central mystical term meaning yieldedness, surrender, or letting go of the self-will</note> into **Desire**, so that it may come into **Being** and **Appearance**. Its will is solely good; it has no other desire than to be only good, gentle, and lovely. No other possibility exists within it, for it is as a **Nothing**, in which no stirring or torment can exist, but is Gentleness itself. 

However, since it cannot be a Nothing because it is a cause and beginning of Desire, it gives itself freely—just as the sunshine gives itself freely into every quality. Thus, the Desire grasps that same **Free-Delight** <note>original: "Freyelust" – the joyful, unconstrained will of the divine</note> as the appearance of the **Abyss** <note>original: "Ungrund" – the groundless, bottomless mystery of God before creation</note> of eternity into itself. It creates within itself a Being according to its own quality. As much quality as there is in the Desire, so much is there also of the Being. 

We must therefore consider it thus: when the Free-Delight yields itself into the hunger of the Desire, the Desire makes an equality out of the free-delight-quality according to Freedom. That is, as if it were nothing, and yet it is; that is, a **Water** and **Oil**. 

But because the Desire—that is, the hunger—is filled with the Free-Delight, it also turns its own quality into Being within the essence of Freedom. Its being is Water, and the being of the Free-Delight is an Oil. Thus, two kinds of quality arise in a single **Spirit**: a fiery one according to the quality of Desire, and a joyful or **Light**-quality according to Freedom.

This interaction creates a dual nature within a single Spirit: one fiery and desiring, the other joyful and light-filled.</summary>

# OF THINGS. CHAPTER VI.

The fiery nature, in its essence—as in its water—gives off a sharpness from the harsh desire; this is salted, or a **salt**, and from the fiery anguish comes a **sulfur**. From these, in the <term>Impression</term> <note>The process of spiritual forces condensing into physical matter</note> and Creation of the world, stones, earths, and metals were formed, as well as elements and stars—all according to the forms within the desire. And the <term>Olitetische</term> <note>original: "Olitetische"; likely a derivative of the Latin 'oleitas' or 'olitetum,' referring to an oily, light-bearing, or balsamic essence</note> nature gives its gentleness as a lovely delight, into which the fiery nature impresses itself with desire and creates physicality. The oily nature pours itself out in its gentleness and creates the vegetable life, such as greening and growing, within the fiery Impression. To this, the fire must give its essence and driving force, acting as the raging sting in the drawing-forth of Desire, which is the divider within physicality—acting as the <term>Distinguisher</term> and the cause of essence and multiplicity.

The Sages have called this form **Mercury**, after the anxious, driving wheel which is the cause of all life and movement, and a master-craftsman within the oily and watery property.

Thus we are to remember and to find the <term>Greatest Mystery</term> <note>original: "Mysterium Magnum"; a central concept in alchemy and mysticism referring to the chaos or "nothingness" from which all things were created</note>, how in all things there is an **Oil, Sulfur, and Salt**, and how they originate. For God has made all things out of Nothing, and that same Nothing is He Himself, as an indwelling Love-Delight, wherein there is no <insert>effect-</insert> <note>The text cuts off mid-word; likely "effectus" or "effecting"</note>

# OF THINGS, CHAPTER VI.

God's essence of love is directed toward [the soul], and its hunger is directed toward that Love; thus, the hunger receives the Love into itself and transforms it into a substantial Being within. In this state, the hunger is no longer a languishing, dark hunger that stings within itself and rages like a poisonous <term>Mercury</term> <note>In alchemy, Mercury represents the volatile, restless, and transformative principle; here it signifies a chaotic or "poisonous" state of the soul before it finds peace.</note>. Instead, a loving desire arises out of the hunger, which is called God's Nature; whereas the hungry, fiery state is called God's Wrath, and in the external nature, it is called a fire.

But in the property of the inner world, where the desire qualifies within the property of free delight, this desire is called the Divine Desire. Within it, the fiery Love burns, and from it, the Kingdom of Joy proceeds. For the free delight gives itself into the harsh desire for this very reason: so that it may produce from itself a fiery Love as a joyful kingdom, which could not exist in the still delight alone. For where there is stillness, there is no joy or movement within.

Thus, the free delight reveals itself as God's property through the fiery property; and the fiery property makes the free delight into the "essence" of the delight, acting as <term>the Oil</term> <note>original: "dz Oele"; in Böhme's thought, "Oil" is the spiritual fuel that allows the light of God to burn without consuming or destroying.</note>, which originates in the impression of <term>the desire</term> <note>original: "d' begierde"</note> as a shine or radiance. <meta>This page continues the discussion of the "Signature of All Things," specifically focusing on the chemical and spiritual properties of matter. The catchword "im" from the previous page leads into the discussion of the "salnitric" (saltpeter-like) shock.</meta>

# ON THE SIGNATURE

The killing within the salnitric <note>original: "Salnitrischen" — referring to the explosive, expansive force of saltpeter, which the author views as a fundamental cosmic energy</note> shock is the common water that flows there. The other corporeal part is the metallic body, derived from the property of the free desire, existing within the compressed form. Furthermore, from the watery property (where the sulfur is in the water) come trees, herbs, and everything that grows in the earthly property, as if in a mortified or dead essence, which nevertheless possesses a silent life, such as a vegetable life <note>original: "vegetabilisches" — referring to the soul-level of plants which grow but do not feel or move like animals</note>.

The oily property <note>original: "Olitetische eigenschafft" — the essence of oil or fat, representing the "soft" or "light" principle in alchemy</note> is also of two kinds, according to the impression <note>original: "Impression" — the process by which spiritual forces compress themselves into physical matter</note>. One part presses back into the desire of freedom, seeking to be free from the wrath of the impression; this is the good spirit, acting as the light within the oil. The other part yields itself into the center of the sulfur's anguish and remains within the corporeality. It adapts itself in every thing according to the property of that thing's salt <note>original: "Saltz" — in Paracelsian alchemy, Salt represents the physical body or stability</note>. Thus, in a fiery salt it is fiery; in a bitter salt, it is bitter; in a harsh salt, harsh; and so forth. 

The first property, according to the light, is sweet in all things. The second property of the oil is according to the form—that is, according to the taste of the thing—whether it be sweet, sour, harsh, or bitter, or however it may be, as is recognized in herbs. In some, there is a bitter poison, and in others, a healing. However, if the poisonous property is [transformed] by the Mercury <note>original: "Mercurium" — the principle of mobility, transformation, and the "word" or "sound" of a substance</note>...

# ON THE NATURE OF THINGS. CHAPTER IV.

The <term>Physician</term><note>original: "Medicus"</note> must know this: that in the strongest <term>Mercury</term><note>In alchemy, Mercury represents the principle of volatility, fluidity, and the "spirit" of matter.</note>, which is the most poisonous of all, lies the highest <term>Tincture</term><note>A potent medicinal or alchemical essence capable of transforming or healing.</note>. However, this does not reside in the inherent property of Mercury itself; it must be "born" within it. For its own property, from its very center, is the anxious life of poison. Yet, it possesses another property within itself, like an **Oil of Light**, from which it derives such strength and power. This is its food and preservation. If this Oil can be separated from the Mercury, it becomes a <term>Tingeing</term><note>The act of transforming or "coloring" a substance into a higher state.</note> and a powerful ignition of all dark lives—that is, of all diseases. For in that same Oil lies the joyful life; it is a hunger for life, such that it ignites the weak and leads them upward.

One will find the highest tincture even in a toad, or an adder, or similar poisonous worms or animals, if one brings them into an oily essence and separates the "wrath" of the Mercury from them. For all life, both external and internal, stands in Spirit and Light. We perceive that God’s wrath and the fire of His anger are the cause of the Divine Joyfulness; likewise, the same is to be recognized in external things. For without the "poisonous" Mercury, all life is mute, loathsome, and regarded as if dead.

Mercury is found in the igniting of the fire; thus, all moving life stands in the fire. And even if a creature were to dwell in the water, the Fire is nonetheless—

It argues that the "poisonous" friction of Mercury is the necessary spark for all life and divine joy.</summary>

### CHAPTER VII.

How Adam was in Paradise and Lucifer was a beautiful angel, and how they were corrupted through imagination and prideful elevation.

## THE PROCESS.

We wish to give the laboratory worker <note>original: "Laboranten"; the practicing alchemist</note> who is an earnest seeker cause for reflection; and if he grasps our meaning, he will surely find the noble Philosopher's Stone <note>original: "Stein der Weisen"; the legendary substance capable of turning base metals into gold and granting eternal life</note>. If he is chosen by God for this, and if his life stands otherwise in the heavenly Mercury—otherwise we remain within the Mystery <note>original: "Mysterium"; the hidden divine secrets of nature</note>.

<meta>This page continues the discussion of the "Signature of All Things," specifically focusing on the fall of the creaturely will and the origin of evil through the metaphor of Mercury.</meta>

# ON THE SIGNATURE

The initial poisonous Mercury <note>In alchemy, Mercury represents the principle of volatility, life-force, and transformation; here, Böhme uses it to describe the driving spirit within a being.</note> in the Devil, as well as in Man and also in the Serpent, shows how a soul may be corrupted. Yet, in doing so, it does not actually corrupt God’s own Being; rather, God’s Being withdraws into itself as if into nothingness. The creaturely Mercury, which arises or is born within the creature at its beginning, goes out of itself—that is, out of the eternal and into time. As it was at the beginning of the creation, this Mercury desires only itself—meaning its own beginning—and wishes to be its own master. It abandons eternity, into which it should have been entirely surrendered <note>original: "gelassen," a key term in German mysticism referring to 'Gelassenheit' or the state of letting go of the self-will to let God act through the soul.</note> with its desire, leading its hunger into that eternity. Had it done so, its poisonous source would not have become manifest.

For whatever hungers after the eternal Nothing—that is, after the still, gentle freedom of God—does not become manifest to itself. Instead, it becomes manifest within that still freedom, just as the hunger is within God’s Being, and the Being is within the hunger. Every hunger or desire creates a "being" or substance within itself according to the quality of that hunger. Thus, the Devil created darkness within himself because he turned with his desire inward, into the property of the Center <term>Centri: the 'Center of Nature,' a point of intense, rotating forces where the material and spiritual worlds meet</term> toward his own desire. He abandoned eternity as the "Nothing"—which is the delight of Love. Therefore, he ignited himself within his own poisonous Mercury—that is, within the forms of life inside himself—and became an anxious torment of fire in darkness, just as wood is consumed by burning.

<summary>Böhme explains the origin of evil as a turning of the inner "Mercury" or life-force away from God's selfless love and toward the self-centered desire of the creature. This internal shift creates a state of spiritual darkness and torment, likened to a fire that consumes itself.</summary>

# OF THINGS, CHAPTER VII.

The Devil's desire [acted] through the serpent: as if through the earthly, deadly property of the serpent, and the fierce, poisonous, living property of God's wrath according to the Devil's own nature; and he was infected in his Divine Oil, that is, in the heavenly essence. There, the Divine Light—which shone out of the Divine Body of the heavenly essence—was extinguished for him. For the curse passed over the soul. Thus, God's cursing is a fleeing: namely, the Divine power which was in the body withdrew into itself, into its own <term>Principle<note>original: "Principium"; referring to the fundamental source or beginning of a thing's existence in Boehme's cosmology</note></term>. And his holy oil (wherein the power of God had dwelt and had created a joy-filled realm like Paradise) became a poison. For the earthly part became manifest after the killing of the fire, acting as the <term>coagulating property<note>original: "cogatrische Eigenschafft"; likely a variation of "coagulative," referring to the hardening or thickening of spirit into matter</note></term>. And immediately <term>Mercury<note>In alchemy, Mercury represents the principle of life, movement, and transformation; here it refers to the "cold" or "dead" state of the life-force after the Fall</note></term>—the coldness in the form of death—took over the government, whereas before he stood as if swallowed up in the heavenly Mercury, in the Divine power. Thus Adam died to God and lived to Death; therefore it was necessary that God should give him birth again. And for that reason the serpent was cursed, because it had served the Devil in obedience.

Thus we understand what lies hidden in the great anguish—as in the strongest Mercury—as an Oil that cures all sickness,

For in the beginning, God created everything good, but through His cursing—or His withdrawing <note>The author plays on the German words "fluchen" (to curse) and "fliehen" (to flee/withdraw)</note>—it became evil. For while God’s desire for love dwelt within the external world-torment and permeated it—just as the sun permeates water or fire permeates iron—the external world was a Paradise. The Divine Essence <term>Essence: the underlying spiritual reality or "being" of a thing</term> sprouted through the earthly essence, and eternal life through that which is mortal. 

But when God cursed the world for the sake of mankind, the mortality became manifest in man and also in the fruit which man was to eat. This quality was previously manifest only in the Tree of Knowledge of Good and Evil, by which Adam and his wife were tested to see whether their desire would enter into Eternity—into God’s being—or into the being of time; into the living oil or the deadly oil; in which torment <note>or "quality of being"</note> the spirit of the soul wished to live—that is, to burn.

Thus, through God’s cursing or withdrawing, the heavenly body was locked away, and the torment of wrath <note>original: "Zornquall"</note> became manifest, and it remains thus locked away. However, because man was placed—in part, in both body and spirit—out of Eternity through the eternal Mercury <note>original: "Mercurium"; here referring to the creative Word or the transformative power of God</note>, that is, through the Word of Divine power, no one could unlock the poisonous death.

# ON THE SIGNATURE

Understand the Father’s property as being shaped into a Word of eternal power within the Father’s fire: The Father, as the shaper of the Father’s fire-form, contains the Divine voice within the delight of freedom essentially <note>original: "essentialiter"; meaning the core essence of being</note>. That is, the property of the Father’s fire turns itself into a <term>Mercury</term> <note>In Boehme's thought, Mercury represents the communicative, mobile, and transformative principle of life</note> of joy within the Divine Being of eternal love. For the Father’s property is the fire-source, and the Son’s property—as the eternal delight—is the love-source. Yet, there would be no desire for Love if the Father’s fire did not kindle it and make it move as a longing: from the fire, the desire arises. 

The Father of all beings gives birth to this holy Desire through his fire-source; this is now his Heart of Love, which gives forth the shine and radiance within his fire. There, from eternity to eternity, the wrath within the fire’s property dies away and is transmuted <note>original: "transmutiret"; an alchemical term for changing one substance into a higher one</note> into a love-desire. 

Therefore, take note: the property of the free delight, here within the property of the fire, is called **Christ**. In the <term>Language of Nature</term> <note>Boehme's concept of a primordial language where the sound of a word reveals the inner essence of the thing named</note>, this means a "Breaker-through": one who takes away the power of the wrath; a shine of Light in the Darkness; a transformation <note>original: "transmutatio"</note> where the love-delight rules over the fire-delight (that is, over the wrath), and the Light rules over the Darkness. Here, the Seed of the Woman (understand: the free delight, in which there is no torment) crushes the wrath of eternal Nature...

# OF THINGS, CHAPTER VII.

Nature is the head of the eternal desire, for the property of fire is rightly called the head, as it is the cause of eternal life. And Liberty—as the free delight or the Nothing—is rightly called the Woman; for in the Nothing, as in the freedom from all torment <note>The author uses "torment" (quall) to describe the restless, driving forces of nature before they find rest in the divine light.</note>, stands the birth of the Holy Trinity of the Deity. Thus, the fire gives life, and the free delight gives being into that life; and within that being is the birth, where the Father, as the eternal foundation, generates his Being—as his Heart—out of the abyss <term>Ungrund: the "abyss" or "groundless," referring to the absolute, undifferentiated nothingness before creation</term> into himself; that is, out of the abyss into himself within a foundation. The Son becomes the Father’s foundation. Thus the Father remains in himself—as far as his property alone is concerned—the foundation of eternal Nature: and the Son remains in the Father as the foundation of power and joy, just as you see a likeness in fire and light. And so the Son tinctures <term>Tincture: a term from alchemy meaning to permeate, transform, or imbue with a new quality</term> the Father with Liberty, as with the Nothing; and the Father tinctures the Son (the Nothing), so that it is an eternal Life within him, and no longer a Nothing, but a sound or voice of the revelation of Eternity.

Therefore, you dear Sages, take note here of what the foundation is, and how you wish to tincture; do not seek the Son outside of the Father when you wish to tincture; there must be a body <note>original: "Corpus"</note>. It was not outside of humanity that the seed of the woman crushed the serpent's head; rather, within humanity, the source of **Divine delight** (understand this as **Love**) revealed itself through an awakening in the human **Essence** <note>German: Essentz; refers to the fundamental spiritual substance of a person</note>. We say this with the foundation of truth, as we have highly perceived it.

Now see, what did Christ do when he was to endure this battle of testing, when the human **essence** <note>original: "Essenz"; the fundamental spiritual substance of a being</note> was to enter again with its desire into the **Godhead**, and eat of God’s bread—that is, of the Divine **Being**? Thus the four properties departed from him; that is, He died to time, to the night, and to the four elements, and stood upon the pure element, and lived for eternity.
