according to the subtlety in the <term>Sulfur and Mercury</term><note>In Paracelsian alchemy, Sulfur represents the soul/flammability and Mercury represents the spirit/fluidity.</note>, according to that same fire it also gives flesh; and according to the anxious darkness, a smoke or soot: but according to the heavenly [nature], as according to the desire of love, a sweet spiritual being; according to the spirit, a praiseworthy scent; and according to the weaving of the fire and light, the element; and from the luster in the <term>fire-crack</term><note>original: "Feuerschracke"; refers to the sudden flash or "shriek" of ignition where spirit manifests into matter.</note> with the glimpse of the light, the noble <term>Tincture</term><note>The highest medicinal or transformative essence.</note>, which tints all oily salts, from which the praiseworthy taste and smell arise.

The individual life—the **human selfhood** <note>original: "Menschliche selbstheit"; refers to the ego or the self-centered will that separates man from God</note>—must pass through death within itself. This is a true archetype: everything that wishes to be free from loathing <note>original: "Eckel"; a term used by Boehme to describe the bitter, conflicting qualities of fallen nature</note> and from the curse must die to the **four elements** <note>the traditional building blocks of the physical world: earth, air, fire, and water</note> within that loathing. Every property is eager for its own likeness; and if it can reach that likeness in **Sulphur** and **Mercury** <term>Sulphur and Mercury: In alchemy, these represent the soul-fire and the spirit-water/movement that constitute all matter</term> through Love, then it carries itself higher than it is in its own degree. 

This is just as the eternal Freedom, with its Delight, introduces a desire into itself through eternal Nature and through fire, and thereby carries itself out much higher in power and majesty. Thus, all things are to be meditated upon, for all things originate from a single Essence. This same Essence is a **mystery of all beings** <note>original: "Mysterium aller Wesen"</note> and a revelation of the **ground of the abyss** <note>original: "vngrundes grund"; the Ungrund is Boehme's term for the absolute, bottomless void or Godhead prior to creation</note>. 

All things are born out of the **Great Mystery** <note>original: "Mysterio magno"; the chaos or divine womb from which all manifestation arises</note>, and one degree proceeds out of another. Now, whatever proceeds forward in its own degree receives no loathing—whether in growing or living things. But whatever enters into itself, into its own selfhood as a private delight, receives a loathing during the passage of the degrees; for every form of nature takes the property of its own kind from the Mystery.

<summary>Boehme explains that all life must undergo a spiritual "death" to its lower elements to reach a higher state of majesty, using alchemical analogies of metals and minerals to describe the soul's refinement.</summary>
<keywords>selfhood, four elements, salnitric seething, Sulphur and Mercury, Great Mystery, Ungrund, spiritual alchemy, purification</keywords>

# OF THINGS, CHAPTER XIV.

property <note>original: "Eygenschafft" — a central term in Böhme’s thought referring to the inherent qualities or "signatures" that define a being's nature</note> in its hunger, and therein it is not tormented, for it is its own property. But if the Will enters back into the birth of the other properties, it receives desire, and desire creates a hunger, and the hunger takes the foreign essence into itself. Now the loathing and the *Turba* <note>original: "Turba" — Latin for "turmoil" or "disturbance"; in this context, it refers to the disruptive force that breaks down a being's natural order</note> are born, for that same Will has entered into a foreign essence against the course of Nature, which is not of its own property. That same foreign essence now rules in the foreign Will and overcomes the Will; now the Will must spit it out, or it will be spit out by the foreign essence itself. Since it cannot remain so, wrath and aversion arise: for the properties run to their center of the first Impression <note>original: "Impression" — the foundational compression or "drawing-in" that forms material existence</note> and seek the fire-strength and power, from which cold and heat arise in the body. They stand against one another as enemies, whereby the first Mother <note>original: "Mutter" — referring to the "Matrix" or the generative source of nature</note> is awakened in her fiercest malice according to the severe Impression. Then the struggle for conquest begins, and whichever property retains the power thrusts the other into the property of death—into the consuming state, into the house of misery.

<summary>This page describes the metaphysical process of how "Turba" or spiritual turmoil is born when the Will seeks properties outside its own nature, leading to internal conflict and physical suffering.</summary>

### CHAPTER XV.

->Concerning the will of the Great Mystery <note>original: "Mysterio magno"</note> in good and evil; from whence a good and an evil will originate: And how one introduces itself into the other.<-

Every quality takes its origin <term>Vhrstand: the primordial source or original state of being</term> from the first, namely from the impression or desire toward nature, as if from the Great Mystery, and leads itself forth as a life out of itself, just as the air goes out from the fire. And everything that proceeds for itself in one will is unsustainable, for it yields itself to no specific quality: from the first origin it dwells only in itself and goes out in one will. This is the true way of eternity in which there is no fragility, provided a thing remains in its own proper quality. For the Great Mystery is from eternity; if the form of the same now goes out from itself and reveals itself out of itself, then that same form stands with its root in the Mystery of eternity. 

However, if the form introduces itself out of itself into another desire, so that two qualities should dwell in one, then there arises from this the opposition <note>original: "Widerwille," implying a contrary will or internal conflict</note> and loathing. For from eternity, only the Element <note>referring here to the "pure element" or the spiritual quintessence, rather than the four material elements</note> and the free delight of eternity existed in the weaving of being. This delight, with its weaving, proceeded from the Great Mystery of eternity as a Spirit, which Spirit is God. But when the Great Mystery moved itself once, and

# OF THINGS, CHAPTER XV.

Once free delight was introduced into the **desire** of the essence, the conflict began within that desire. For there, within the desire, the four elements arose out of the single Element <note>The "Pure Element" or "Quintessence" which Böhme believes preceded the four material elements.</note>—which carries only one will. These four different desires and wills now rule within a single **body**, creating adversity and strife: heat against cold, fire against water, air against earth. Each is the death and destruction of the other.

Thus, the creature that exists under this government is nothing but an internal dying and a conflict; it is an enmity within itself. No counsel can be given to it unless it enters again into a single will. This cannot happen unless the multiplicity of wills is broken, and the desire from which the four elements arise dies away completely. This must occur so that the will may again become what it was from eternity.

In this, we humans recognize what we are under the government of the four elements: nothing other than a conflict and a reluctance, a self-enmity, a desire for loathing, and a delight in death. For the delight that arises from such desire must die. This will—which proceeded from the **Great Mystery** <note>original: "Mysterio"; refers to the *Mysterium Magnum*, the chaos or "Ungrund" from which God brings forth the manifestation of the universe.</note> from eternity, and which the **Spirit of God** breathed into the **human image** as a likeness of God—must become free from loathing and reluctance; thus must the

# ON THE SIGNATURE

The desire of the four elements <note>The physical world composed of earth, air, fire, and water</note> must die, and the will must enter again into the eternal element. It must once more accept the law of eternity and move and proceed within a single element, just as God had originally created it. Man set himself against this and introduced himself into the dominion of the four elements, in which he inherited death for himself, as well as the conflict of the forms of life. From this arises their sickness, loathing, and aversion. For everything that lives in God's will is either not originated in its own desire, or if it is originated therein, it has died to that self-desire. Every will that enters into its own selfhood <note>German: *selbstheit*; the state of being focused only on the ego</note> and seeks the ground of its own life-form, breaks itself off from the Great Mystery <note>Latin: *Magnum Mysterium*; the hidden foundation of all reality</note> and enters into a thing of its own. It wishes to be its own dominion, and thus it is contrary to the first Mystery, for that Mystery alone is everything. Then the child is recognized as evil, for it strives in disobedience against its own mother who gave it birth. But if the child leads its will and desire back into that from which it was born and originated, it is entirely one with the same, and can be disturbed by nothing; for it enters into the "Nothing" <note>The divine void or stillness beyond physical existence</note>, into the very Essence from which it first proceeded.

->Therefore, O Man, understand what you must do.<-

# OF THINGS, CHAPTER XV.

Look into yourself and see what you are: whether you stand in the **resignation** <note>original: "Gelassenheit" — a central concept in German mysticism referring to a state of letting go of the self-will to become receptive to God</note> of your Mother (from whom you were born and created in the beginning), and whether you are inclined toward that same will. If not, then know that you are an apostate, disobedient child, and have made yourself an enemy; for you have entered into your **own desire and will** and have made yourself into a **possession** <note>original: "eygenthumb" — implying a state of self-ownership or being "self-possessed" rather than belonging to the divine whole</note>. Thus, you cannot dwell in the first Mother, but only in yourself. For your will has entered into **selfhood** <note>original: "selbstheit" — the state of being centered on the ego</note>, and everything that sickens and distresses you is your own selfhood; you make yourself your own enemy and lead yourself into your own death.

Now, if you wish to go out from death again, you must entirely abandon your **own desire** <note>original: "eygene Begierde"</note> which leads itself into foreign essences, and you must become as a **Nothing** within selfhood and own-desire, so that you no longer will or desire for yourself. Instead, you must lead your desire back entirely, through resignation, into the Eternal—as into God's will—so that this same Will may be your will and desire. Outside of this is nothing but misery and death, a constant dying and corruption. For from this arises the **Election of Grace** <note>original: "Gnadenwahl" — the theological concept of being chosen by God's mercy</note>: when the human will, which came out of the **Unity of Eternity**, has entered into a "self" as its own lust and desire—if it then breaks itself away from selfhood again,

<summary>This page explores the mystical concept of 'Gelassenheit' (resignation), arguing that human suffering and spiritual death arise from the ego's desire for self-possession, which can only be remedied by returning the will to the divine Unity.</summary>
<keywords>resignation, selfhood, ego, divine will, election of grace, unity of eternity, spiritual rebirth, mysticism</keywords>

and enters into the dying of the self-will, and leads his desire back alone into the first Mother, then the first Mother chooses him again as a child and makes him one with the eternal will of eternity. But he who remains in selfhood <term>Selbstheit: the state of being centered on one's own ego rather than God</term>, he remains in eternal dying, as if in an eternal self-enmity; and that alone is called sin, because it is an enmity against God, in which the creature wants to be its own government <note>original: "Regiment" - meaning its own ruler or authority</note>. Thus, in its selfhood, as in a contentious government, it can neither will nor do anything good. And just as it does nothing to itself but inflict, awaken, and make active <note>original: "qualificirende" - a term used by Boehme to describe the active qualities or powers of a being</note> dying and death, so also can it do nothing else to its fellow members. For from this also originates lies: that the creature denies the unity with the will of God and sets its selfhood in its place; that it goes out from the unity into desire and its own lust. If it recognized that all being belonged to the Mother who bore it, and did not consider the being of the Mother as its own doing, but as common to all, then there would arise no greed, envy, strife, and ill-will, from which wrath arises as the fire of destruction.

All sins arise out of selfhood. For selfhood swings itself with desire into its own; it makes itself into greed.

# OF MATTERS, CHAPTER XV.

and envy; it draws foreign essences into its own desire and turns the possessor of that foreign essence into an enemy against itself, so that sin is worked with sin, and loathing with loathing, and everything runs in and among one another as a vain abomination of the eternal Mother <note>The "eternal Mother" in this context refers to the primordial source or the "Ungrund" from which all existence emerges in Boehme's theosophy</note>.

In the same way, we must consider the reborn will <term>reborn will: a will that has been spiritually transformed to seek God rather than the self</term>, which enters back out of its "I-ness" or selfhood into yieldedness <term>yieldedness: from the German 'Gelassenheit', meaning a state of surrender to God's will</term>. It also becomes an enemy and an object of loathing to selfhood, just as sickness is an enemy to health, and conversely, health is an enemy to sickness. Thus, the yielded will and the self-will are in constant enmity—an everlasting war and strife. The self-will seeks only what serves its own selfhood, but the yielded will cares for nothing for itself; instead, it leads its desire solely and alone into its Eternal Mother, so that it may be one with her. It desires to be nothing, so that the Mother alone may be everything within it. The self-will says to the yielded will: "You are foolish to give yourself up to death, when you could live heartily within me." But the yielded will speaks: "You are my loathing, pain, and contrary-will; you lead me out of eternity into time, into nothing but sorrow and misery. You sicken me for a time, and then you give my body to the earth and the soul to Hell."

And if he does anything good, he does it not out of his own self-will, but rather the will resigned in God compels him so that he must do what he himself does not wish to do. And when he now does it, he does it only as an <term>instrument (werckzeug)</term> of the resigned will—not out of his own desire, but out of God’s will, which guides the resigned will within its desire as a tool.

# OF THE NATURE OF THINGS, CHAPTER XV.

Therefore, whoever wishes to behold the Kingdom of God and arrive there must lead his soul out of selfhood <note>original: "selbstheit"; a central term in Boehme's thought referring to the egoistic, self-centered nature of the fallen human will</note> and out of earthly desire. Just as a physician conducts the cure of a disease out of a painful craving and leads it into a lovely desire, so then the cure of the disease in the body also leads out of the painful craving and into a lovely desire: the disease becomes the servant of the medicine. In the same way, the earthly evil will—when the soul's will is cured—becomes the servant of the resigned will <note>original: "gelassenen willens"; referring to 'Gelassenheit' or the state of yieldedness and surrender to God</note>.

The elemental and sidereal man <note>The "elemental" refers to the physical body made of the four elements, while the "sidereal" refers to the "starry" or astral spirit that governs human impulses and intellect</note> should only be the instrument with which the soul-man works in the resigned will; for God created him for that purpose. But in Adam, the soul made him the master and installed him, and entered into his prison, and surrendered her will into it. If she is to be recognized as God's child, however, she must die to that again, and completely die away in God's will—in the death of Christ—within the earthly selfhood and desire. She must be born entirely anew in God's will, and take the power away from the earthly will in its selfhood, and rule over it and keep it under constraint, as a master does his tool. Then selfhood loses its power and the lust of selfhood passes away.

# ON THE SIGNATURE

as he was before he became a <term>creature</term><note>original: "Creatur"; referring to a created being or any part of the created order</note>: and in the will into which God created him; and he is an instrument in the resonance of God, upon which only the Spirit of God’s will strikes, to His honor and wondrous deeds.

He understands nothing of the form of the expressed word as it stands in its own foundation, for he is only born into the form from the outside, and not in the power of the <term>Universal Mother</term><note>original: "Allgebährerin"; the creative, generative power of God that gives birth to all existence</note>, whose foundation has neither beginning, enclosure, nor end.

He who is born from within, out of the speaking resonance of God into the Spirit of God’s will, travels everywhere freely in the <term>ground and the abyss</term><note>original: "grund vnd vngrund"; "Ungrund" is Boehme's term for the primordial, bottomless void or the absolute nothingness out of which God and the world emerge</note>, and is bound to no form; for he does not travel in the selfhood, but the eternal Will leads him as its tool, according to what pleases God.

# ON THE SIGNATURE

<note>original: "DE SIGNATURA" - likely referring to the "Signature of All Things," the author's theory that the inner essence of things is revealed in their outward form.</note>

...to be a [spoiled] child, and not die to his <term>Selfhood (Selbstheit) <note>The ego or the state of being centered on one's own selfish desires rather than God.</note></term> in grace, and become a child of grace within the <term>resigned will (gelassenen Willen) <note>A central concept in German mysticism meaning a state of letting go, surrender, and yielding one's will to God.</note></term>.

Everything that is taught concerning <term>Christ’s atonement (Christignugthunung) <note>The theological concept of Christ satisfying God's justice on behalf of humanity.</note></term> and finding comfort in the <term>suffering of Christ (Leyden Christi)</term>—if it does not also teach the true foundation of how one must die to Selfhood in Christ’s death and [live] in the resigned will, entirely in obedience to God as a new child of a new will—that is merely external. It is not within the speaking resonance of God, which is the door of Christ. No, hypocrisy and [false] comfort help nothing; rather, one must die to the false will and desire in Christ’s death, and rise in Him in a completely resigned will in <term>Christ’s Resurrection (Christi Aufferstehung)</term>. One must continually kill the earthly selfhood and dampen the evil that the earthly will introduces into its <insert>sinful</insert> desires, like an evil fire that always wants to burn. To merely comfort oneself and place the suffering of Christ at the forefront is not the true Faith. No, no! That is only on the outside and not within. Rather, [true faith] is a <term>converted will (vmbgekehrter wille)</term> that enters into repentance for its earthly wickedness and wants no more of it, yet finds that it is held captive by narrow earthly desires. With this converted will, out of loathing for these false desires, the soul casts itself entirely into <term>God’s Mercy (Gottes Erbarmen)</term> with great, anxious longing, into Christ’s obedience, suffering, and death; and to the earthly desire...

<summary>The author argues that true Christianity requires an internal death of the ego and a total surrender to God's will, rather than a superficial reliance on the doctrine of Christ's atonement. # OF THINGS, CHAPTER XV.

The pleasure in the reversed will dies away entirely in Christ's death; he who does not wish to come out of Christ's death again, who cries out forever: *Abba*, dear Father <note>original: "Abba, lieber Vatter" — a reference to Romans 8:15, expressing the intimate relationship between the believer and God</note>, take your Son's obedience for me into yourself; let me live in you only in his obedience within his death; let me die in him, so that I am nothing in myself, but live and am in his will, in his humanity within you. Take me in entirely in his resurrection, and not me in my unworthiness, but me in him: Let me be dead in myself, and give me his life, so that I am your obedient son in him, so that his suffering and death may be mine, and that I may be that same Christ who has taken the power from death in him, as a little branch of his life, before you.

And thus, and in no other way, is the true Christian faith; it is not only a comforter, but an everlasting desire: the desire reaches the suffering of Christ, which would always gladly be obedient, if it only knew how it should conduct itself before him; which constantly falls down before him, and sinks itself into the highest humility before him; which gladly suffers and does everything, only so that it may receive grace; which is willing to take the cross of Christ upon itself, and of all

# ON THE SIGNATURE

The world in its <term>selfhood</term><note>German: "Selbstheit"; refers to the ego-driven state of being separated from God</note> mocks and regards nothing, but instead constantly presses into the desire of Christ’s love. This desire alone grows out of Christ’s death and out of his resurrection in God, and it brings forth fruits in patience which are hidden in God. Of these, the earthly man knows nothing, for he remains within his own selfhood. A true Christian is a constant knight and enters entirely into Christ’s person in will and desire (just as He walked upon the earth). When Christ walked on earth, He willed to overcome death and to lead human selfhood into true <term>resignation</term><note>German: "Gelassenheit"; a central mystical concept meaning yieldedness, surrender, or letting go of the self-will</note> in obedience to God. A true Christian desires to do the same; he desires constantly to die to the malice of death and wrath, to submit himself in obedience, and to rise and live in God through Christ’s own obedience.

Therefore, dear brothers, beware of putting on <term>Christ’s purple robe</term><note>A reference to the robe placed on Jesus during his passion; here it symbolizes a superficial or hypocritical claim to faith without inner change</note> without a resigned will. The poor sinner, without repentance for his sin and without a turning of his will, puts it on only to mock Christ. Beware of the doctrine that teaches of one's own ability and of the works of justification.

# OF MATTERS, CHAPTER XV.

The expressed form—which has introduced itself into a <term>self-hood</term><note>original: "selbstheit"; refers to the egoistic state of being separated from God's will</note> and has led itself away from God—whatever does not serve before God in the <term>office of a servant</term><note>original: "Knechts Ampte"; the state of humble submission to the divine</note> is entirely false, whether it be highly or lowly learned, or unlearned. We are altogether merely servants of the great God; nothing leads into its own possession unless it be born in God's wrath within the <term>impression of nature</term><note>original: "Impression der Natur"; the physical or material contraction of spiritual forces into a rigid state</note>. And even if a Christian possesses something of his own, which is not false in itself, he is nevertheless only a servant within it, acting as a <term>distributor</term><note>original: "Außtheiler"</note> for his Lord and a guardian of the Lord’s work. He acts therein for his Lord and not for his self-hood alone. Everything that he thinks to introduce, and does introduce, into his self-hood, he leads into the anxious chest of <term>avarice</term><note>original: "Geitz"</note>, envy, and the self-indulgence of the flesh, as if into a vessel apostatized from God—into the impression of nature—and he steals it away from his Lord, who appointed him as a <term>steward</term><note>original: "Verwalter"</note>. He is a thief of God and of His essence, let him excuse himself however he wishes.

A true Christian recognizes himself as a servant of God, who is commanded to deal rightly with God’s works. It is not his own, for even in this earthly work of this <term>tabernacle</term><note>original: "Hütten"; a biblical metaphor for the physical body</note> he is not at home. Whether he seeks, plants, builds, trades, or does whatever he wishes, he should always know that he does it for God.

Reason contradicts this without sufficient knowledge and says: "God is almighty and omniscient; He made it and has done with His work what He wills; who will contend with the Most High?" Yes, dear Reason <note>The author personifies 'Vernunfft' (Reason) as a limited human faculty that fails to grasp spiritual depths.</note>, now you have caught the scent; first learn the ABCs in the Great Mystery <note>original: "Mysterio," referring to the *Mysterium Magnum*, the chaos or void from which God brings forth all things</note>. The first Mystery in which the creature stands is the all-present Mystery: and the other in the outgoing spirit is its own possession and its own will.) For every angel has its own spirit, which is born out of its own Mystery, which has its origin from eternity. Why then does that same spirit become a tempter of God?

But whether Esau remained in sin, I do not know, nor does the Scripture say so. The blessing belonged to Esau, that is, to Adam; but he forfeited it through the Fall, and so the blessing fell upon Jacob, that is, upon Christ. # OF THE NATURE OF THINGS, CHAPTER XVI.

the door of grace stood open in Jacob—that is, in Christ. Thus Jacob—that is, Christ—spoke afterward, when he entered into Adam’s soul and flesh: "Come all to me, you who are weary and laden with sins; I will refresh you." <note>Matthew 11:28</note> Likewise: "I have come to call poor sinners to repentance," <note>Matthew 9:13</note> not Jacob, who has no need of it, but rather Esau, who does need it. And when he (Esau) has come, Christ says: "There is joy in heaven over him, more than over ninety-nine righteous ones who need no repentance" <note>Luke 15:7</note>—more than over ninety-nine Jacobs, who are in the lineage of Christ and were grasped in the center of life’s rising. Over one poor sinner, whom Wrath has grasped in the center of God’s fierceness at the beginning of life, and who is elected to damnation—there is joy if he enters again into the dying of sins with the sin of death, more than over ninety-nine righteous ones who need no repentance.

But who are the righteous? For in Adam we have all become sinners. They are those whom the lineage of Christ <note>original: "Lineam Christi"</note> grasps in the humanity at the rising of life; not that they cannot fall, as Adam did, but because they have been grasped in Christ’s spirit of will in the wrestling wheel <note>A signature Böhmean term referring to the turbulent interaction of spiritual forces</note>—where Love and Wrath stand in equal balance—and were elected to life: such as Jacob,

<summary>This page provides a mystical interpretation of the biblical figures Jacob and Esau, representing the "lineage of Christ" and the "sinner elected to wrath" respectively, emphasizing that heaven rejoices more when a "lost" soul repents.</summary>
<keywords>Jacob, Esau, Christ, Adam, repentance, election, wrath of God, center of life, lineage of Christ, wrestling wheel</keywords>

--- Page 401 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of the spiritual lineages of the Old Testament patriarchs, specifically contrasting the line of grace with the line of wrath.</meta>

-># ON THE SIGNATURE<-

just as it happened to Isaac and also to Abel: But this <term>Lineage</term><note>original: "Linea"; referring to the spiritual succession of the promise</note> was intended to be the preacher and teacher to Cain, Ishmael, and Esau, and to admonish them toward <term>repentance</term><note>original: "Buße"</note> and the turning away from <term>Wrath</term><note>original: "Zorn"; in Böhme's theology, the fiery, judgmental quality of God</note>. This same Lineage was meant to enter into Adam, Cain, Ishmael, and Esau within their Wrath (which was ignited within them), and to break the devil’s sting with Love, so that Cain, Ishmael, and Esau had an open door to <term>Grace</term><note>original: "Gnade"</note> if they wished to turn back and die in Jacob—that is, to enter into the death of Christ—and to die to sin in Abel, Isaac, Jacob, and Christ; thus they would have been received into the <term>Election</term><note>original: "außerwehlung"; the divine choosing of the soul</note> in grace.

Jacob took Esau’s place in the blessing; why did that happen? In Jacob was the promised <term>Seed</term><note>original: "Sahme"</note> of Abraham and Adam; from this Lineage the blessing was meant to come upon the sinful Adam and Esau. Jacob had to be filled with God’s blessing so that he might bless the <term>Firstborn</term><note>original: "Erstgebohrnen"</note>—the angry Adam and Esau. For within our flesh and soul, the blessing was to be born as Christ, so that the Seed of the Woman could crush the head of the Serpent. Within humanity, the Wrath had to be drowned and stilled: No mere sacrifice could do it, but rather a self-surrender into the <term>Fury</term><note>original: "Grim"; the harsh, dark aspect of the divine fire</note>, so that Love might drown the Fury.

<summary>Jacob Boehme explains that the spiritual lineage of Jacob exists to offer redemption to the 'angry' lineage of Esau, illustrating how divine Love must enter and neutralize the Wrath within human nature. He interprets the biblical struggle for the birthright as a cosmic process where the blessing of Christ must be born within the flesh to overcome the serpent's influence.</summary>

<keywords>Jacob, Esau, Wrath and Love, Lineage of Grace, Repentance, Seed of the Woman, Atonement, Jacob Boehme, Mystical Theology</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the theological discussion of the biblical brothers Jacob and Esau as archetypes for the struggle between the "new man" (Christ) and the "old man" (Adam/sinful nature).</meta>

# OF THINGS, CHAPTER XVI.

Jacob, in Christ, had to drown Esau in his own blood through the power of love, so that Esau might also become a Jacob in Christ. However, if Esau had refused to accept his brother Jacob and continued to quarrel over the birthright—that is, the Adam in sins—he neither wants to nor can accept Christ, for he must die to the sinful flesh and will. Therefore, Esau has always waged war against Jacob: for Jacob was meant to drown him in his blood within Christ. The evil Adam within Esau did not want this; he wanted to live in his own selfhood <note>The German "Selbstheit" refers to the egoistic, self-centered nature of fallen humanity</note>. Therefore, he fought with the earthly Adam against Jacob.

But when Jacob went to meet him with his gifts—that is, when Christ came into humanity with his gift of love—then Esau fell upon his brother Jacob’s neck and wept. For when Christ entered into humanity, then Adam in Esau wept, and he repented of his sins and of his intention to kill Jacob. For when God's love entered into the wrath of God within humanity, then the wrathful Father repented of our sin and misery. And Jacob, with his humility, drove the weeping tears out of his brother Esau; that is, the love in the humanity of Christ drove out great mercy from and through the wrathful Father, so that the wrathful Father, in the midst

<summary>This page provides a mystical interpretation of the reconciliation between Jacob and Esau, viewing it as a metaphor for Christ's love overcoming the wrathful, sinful nature of Adam. It describes the transformation of the "selfhood" through humility and divine mercy.</summary>

<keywords>Jacob and Esau, Christology, Adam, repentance, divine wrath, selfhood, reconciliation, mysticism</keywords>

--- Page 403 ---
<meta>This page continues the theological discussion of divine wrath and mercy, specifically focusing on the mechanics of salvation and the role of the human will in the process of election.</meta>

# ON THE SIGNATURE

In his ignited wrath, he unlocked an open door of mercy within humanity for Adam and all his children; for his Love broke through the wrath—placing itself into death—and made an open portal within death toward his grace for poor sinners. Now, it is said of the poor sinner, whom wrath has chosen for the condemnation of eternal death, that he must enter into that very death and die to sin within the death of Christ; then Christ drowns the sin in his blood and chooses him again as a child of God.

Here is the calling: Christ calls us into his death, into his dying. The sinner does not want this, and so a struggle arises within the sinner between the seed of the woman and the seed of the serpent <note>A reference to Genesis 3:15, interpreted here as the struggle between the divine spark and the fallen nature within a person</note>. Whichever side now triumphs receives the child. Now the free will may reach toward whichever side it desires: both portals stand open to it. Many who are even within the lineage of Christ are led into wickedness through worldly lust, just as Adam was; they are indeed called, but they do not persevere in the election. For the election <term>Election: the process by which a soul is chosen or "elected" by God for salvation</term> passes over the one who departs from sin. He is chosen who dies to sin in Christ’s death and rises in Christ’s resurrection—the one who accepts God in Christ, not with the mouth alone, but in divine desire within the will and the very act of being.

<summary>This page describes the internal struggle for salvation, explaining how Christ's love transformed death into a gateway for mercy and emphasizing that "election" depends on the soul's active will to die to sin.</summary>
<keywords>mercy, wrath of God, free will, election, seed of the woman, spiritual rebirth, atonement, Adam</keywords>

# OF THE NATURE OF THINGS, CHAPTER XVI.

Birth, as a new birth of fire. Knowledge alone does not grasp it; only the earnest desire and the breaking of the sinful will can comprehend it.

Thus it is with the <term>Election of Grace</term> <note>German: Gnadenwahl; the theological concept of Predestination or God's sovereign choice of who receives salvation</note>, as reason perceives it, there is no sufficient ground: Adam is chosen in Christ, but that many a branch on the tree withers is not the fault of the tree, for it does not withhold its sap from any branch; rather, the branch gives itself too much outward with its desire; it runs in its own will, as if seized by the ignition of the sun and the fire, before it can recover and refresh itself again in its mother's sap. So also man perishes under the malice of company on evil ways: God offers him His grace that he should do penance: But company and the Devil lead him upon the path of the ungodly, until he is caught too hard in <term>Wrath</term> <note>In this context, "Wrath" refers to the dark, fiery principle of nature separated from God's light</note>; then it becomes difficult: He would indeed be called, but he is evil; God chooses for Himself only His children: Because he is evil, the Election passes over him; but if he becomes pious again, the Election catches him again. Thus says the <term>Scripture</term> <note>A reference to Matthew 22:14: "For many are called, but few are chosen"</note>: Many are called, but when the Election in Christ's suffering and death passes over them, they are (because of their own conceived evil will) not capable of it; so they are then

<summary>This page explains the Election of Grace using the metaphor of a tree and its branches, arguing that spiritual failure results from a branch's own "outward desire" rather than a lack of divine sap. It emphasizes that while many are called, an individual's stubborn will can make them incapable of receiving the Election.</summary>

Therefore, whoever blames God despises His mercy, which He has introduced into humanity, and brings the judgment upon his own neck—yes, upon himself in body and soul.

Thus, I wish to have faithfully warned the reader and placed before his eyes what the Lord of all beings has given to me. He may look at himself from within and from without in this mirror, and so he will find who he is. Every reader will find his benefit therein, whether he be good or evil.
